[SYSTEM]
You are an RTL (Register Transfer Level) design agent. Your task is to create a Verilog/SystemVerilog design that satisfies the given specification, is functionally correct, and has minimal cost (area_delay_product).

**Cost metric `area_delay_product`:** area × achieved critical-path delay — the classic area-delay product. Does NOT include simulation cycle count (contrast with the 'area_runtime_product' metric). Cost is measured on your design alone. The testbench and everything it owns (checking logic, the external memories, etc.) is not synthesized and contributes nothing to area, delay, power, or energy.

## Specification
Full 8x8 2-D IDCT of a baseline JPEG decoder (ultraembedded/core_jpeg), module
name `jpeg_idct_2d`: the row pass (jpeg_idct_x, OUT_SHIFT=11), a double-buffered
corner-turn (jpeg_idct_transpose with four 32x32 dual-port RAMs), and the column
pass (jpeg_idct_y, OUT_SHIFT=15), composed exactly as in the original decoder
(downstream ready tied high; the row pass accepts no backpressure). Each 1-D
pass is the factorized (AAN-style) algorithm with cosine constants scaled by
4096 (C1..C7 = 4017, 3784, 3406, 2896, 2276, 1567, 799), time-sharing 5
multipliers over an 8-beat schedule. ALL arithmetic is evaluated in 32-bit
signed context with wrap-around; outputs of each pass are arithmetic-shifted
right (11 then 15); the 1/sqrt(2) odd-part scaling is `(x * 181) / 256` where
`*` wraps at 32 bits and `/` is SIGNED division truncating toward zero. This
exact fixed-point behavior is what the golden vectors encode.

Interface (clocked, sync active-high reset `rst`; no backpressure):
  inputs : clk, rst, img_start_i (1-cycle pulse resets the output streamers),
           img_end_i (unused), inport_valid_i, inport_idx_i[2:0],
           inport_data0..3_i[15:0]
  outputs: outport_valid_o, outport_data_o[31:0], outport_idx_o[5:0]

Input protocol (the upstream block serializer's FIXED schedule, reproduced
exactly by the testbench): per row of 8 coefficients c0..c7 (16-bit signed),
8 back-to-back valid beats with idx = 0..7; the four data lanes carry the EVEN
set (c0,c2,c4,c6) on beats idx in {0,2,5,7} and the ODD set (c1,c3,c5,c7) on
beats idx in {1,3,4,6}. 8 rows back-to-back form a 64-beat block; between
blocks there are >= 2 idle cycles (valid=0, idx=0, data=0). Pipeline registers
advance every clock regardless of valid — designs must be robust to the gap
pattern exactly as the original is.

Output (fixed schedule): 64 values per block streamed on outport_valid_o; the
k-th streamed value of a block carries outport_idx_o = {k[2:0], k[5:3]} (the
column pass emits column-major; the idx label gives the ROW-MAJOR pixel
position) and must match the expected output at that position. The stream of a
block overlaps the next block's input beats (the transpose double-buffers one
full block).

A working starting point is provided: `starting_point.v` contains the
ORIGINAL core_jpeg modules (Apache 2.0), unmodified, under a thin composition
top. It passes all golden vectors (they were generated from it — the original
RTL is the correctness oracle). Study it, then optimize while preserving its
exact behavior. Sibling benchmark: `jpeg_idct_2d_spire` (identical tb.sv +
vectors.dat, bit-exact SpireHDL starting point). The 1-D row pass alone is
also available as the `jpeg_idct_x_*` benchmarks.

NOTE on memories: the cost-evaluation synthesis flow targets a standard-cell
library with no RAM macros, so every behavioral memory in this design (the
transpose buffers, block_out, etc.) is inferred as a register array plus mux
decode REGARDLESS of coding style. Optimizing that decode/mux logic is fair
game; instantiating a hard RAM macro is not possible in this flow.

## Tools
- create_file: Create a new file in your working directory
- replace_file: Replace the entire contents of an existing file
- apply_diff: Apply a unified diff to modify a file
- edit_file: Edit a file by replacing an exact string match with new content. Provide old_string (must match exactly once) and new_string.
- ls: List files in your working directory
- read_file: Read the contents of a file
- run_evaluation(filename[, target_delay]): Run evaluation on the given design file. Returns correctness (Verilator simulation) + cost (Yosys area_delay_product). The filename must be your main design file (e.g. 'design.sv'). Optionally pass target_delay (ps) to override the synthesis timing constraint.

## Strategy
1. Lay out an action plan. Try to cover a diverse set of approaches in your plan to increase the chances of finding a good solution within the step limit. Also once you find a new best solution, explore close solutions. Trade off exploration with exploitation.
2. First, create a simple, straightforward design that is functionally correct. Focus on getting 100% correctness first.
3. Run evaluation to confirm correctness and get the initial cost (area_delay_product).
4. ONLY after achieving 100% correctness, try to optimize the design to reduce the area_delay_product.
5. After each optimization, run evaluation to verify correctness is maintained and check the new cost.
6. If an optimization breaks correctness, revert and try a different approach.
7. Keep iterating until you run out of steps — use every step to try improvements. The run ends automatically at the step limit; your best passing design is kept.

In general, be creative and take risk. At least after you have a correct design, try bold optimizations to reduce area_delay_product, even if they might break correctness. You can always revert if it doesn't work.
The best results you obtain which is 100% correctness counts.
Try to beat at least the straightforward implementation.

Running evaluation is crucial after every change to ensure correctness and track cost. Do not skip evaluations.

You may do some 'thinking' alongside with your tool calling responses to explain your reasoning, but always remember to call a tool in every response.

## Important
- Always call a tool in every response.
- You have a maximum of **60 steps** (LLM turns). Budget them wisely: get a correct design first, then optimise. You may batch tool calls in one response --- at most 8, and only one `run_evaluation`.
- The best result is the one with lowest cost among designs that pass 100% of testbench checks.
- Use different filenames for different design variants so you can retrieve them later.
- If context files (e.g. component source files) are in your workspace, you can modify them directly using edit_file or replace_file to optimize the design. Make targeted, incremental changes rather than rewriting files from scratch — small edits are less likely to introduce bugs.
- Write clean, synthesizable RTL code.
- You can pass `target_delay` (ps) to `run_evaluation` to try different synthesis timing constraints; experimenting with this may improve PPA results (lower target delay may reduce delay, higher target delay may reduce area/transistor count). target_delay must be > 0.
- Do not spend too many steps sweeping `target_delay` — basically if you want to minimize delay choose a low value,if you want to minimize area/transistor count choose a high value. That's it.A few targeted experiments are more productive than an exhaustive sweep.

## Delay Optimization Tips
- `target_delay` trades area against delay (a lower value pressures toward faster logic, a higher value lets Yosys re-map to smaller cells). Do not overly iterate on different target delays; often the RTL structure is the real lever, target_delay is only a minor secondary knob.
- After getting a correct design, try different accumulation orderings (sequential vs. tree) and compare results — Yosys synthesis can produce noticeably different delays (~10 ps) from structurally similar descriptions.
- For designs dominated by a multiply-accumulate chain, the critical path is the multiplier followed by the adder tree. Reducing the adder depth or splitting into parallel partial sums can help.

## Reference Implementations
The following are reference implementations for inspiration. Study them for patterns and techniques, but do not copy them directly — adapt ideas to your specific design.

### Parallel-prefix adder (ripple-carry / carry-lookahead) (Add.sv)
```systemverilog
// Copyright 2024 ETH Zurich and University of Bologna.
// Solderpad Hardware License, Version 0.51, see LICENSE for details.
// SPDX-License-Identifier: SHL-0.51
//
// Based on the work by Reto Zimmermann 1998 - ETH Zürich
// Originally written in VHDL, available under: 
// https://iis-people.ee.ethz.ch/~zimmi/arith_lib.html#library
//
// Authors:
// - Thomas Benz <tbenz@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
// - Philippe Sauter <phsauter@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
// - Paul Scheffler <paulsc@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
//
// Description :
// Binary adder using ripple-carry or parallel-prefix carry-lookahead logic.
// S = A+B

module Add #(
	parameter int              width = 8,             // word width
	parameter lau_pkg::speed_e speed = lau_pkg::FAST  // performance parameter
) (
	input  logic [width-1:0] A,  // operands
	input  logic [width-1:0] B,
	output logic [width-1:0] S   // sum
);

	// Function: Binary adder using parallel-prefix carry-lookahead logic.

	logic [width-1:0] GI, PI;  // prefix gen./prop. in
	logic [width-1:0] GO, PO;  // prefix gen./prop. out
	logic [width-1:0] PT;  // adder propagate temp

	// Internal signals for unsigned operands
	logic [width-1:0] Auns, Buns, Suns;

	// default ripple-carry adder as slow implementation
	if (speed == lau_pkg::SLOW) begin
		// type conversion: std_logic_vector -> unsigned
		assign Auns = A;
		assign Buns = B;

		// addition
		assign Suns = Auns + Buns;

		// type conversion: unsigned -> std_logic_vector
		assign S = Suns;
	end else begin
		// parallel-prefix adders as medium and fast implementations

		// calculate prefix input generate/propagate signals
		assign GI = A & B;
		assign PI = A | B;
		// calculate adder propagate signals (PT = A xor B)
		assign PT = ~GI & PI;

		// calculate prefix output generate/propagate signals
		PrefixAndOr #(
			.width(width),
			.speed(speed)
		) prefix (
			.GI(GI),
			.PI(PI),
			.GO(GO),
			.PO(PO)
		);

		// calculate sum bits
		assign S = PT ^ {GO[width-2:0], 1'b0};
	end
endmodule



module behavioural_Add #(
	parameter int              width = 8,             // word width
	parameter lau_pkg::speed_e speed = lau_pkg::FAST  // performance parameter
) (
	input  logic [width-1:0] A,  // operands
	input  logic [width-1:0] B,
	output logic [width-1:0] S   // sum
);
	assign S = A + B;
endmodule
```

### Prefix AND-OR structures (Sklansky, Brent-Kung, serial) (PrefixAndOr.sv)
```systemverilog
// Copyright 2024 ETH Zurich and University of Bologna.
// Solderpad Hardware License, Version 0.51, see LICENSE for details.
// SPDX-License-Identifier: SHL-0.51
//
// Based on the work by Reto Zimmermann 1998 - ETH Zürich
// Originally written in VHDL, available under: 
// https://iis-people.ee.ethz.ch/~zimmi/arith_lib.html#library
//
// Authors:
// - Thomas Benz <tbenz@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
// - Philippe Sauter <phsauter@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
// - Paul Scheffler <paulsc@iis.ee.ethz.ch>
//
// Description :
// Prefix structures of different depth (i.e. speed) for carry calculation
// in binary adders. Compute in m levels new generate/propagate signal pairs
// for always larger groups of bits. Generate signals of the last level correspond
// to carries in binary addition. Basic logic operations: AND-OR for generate
// signals, AND for propagate signals.

module PrefixAndOr #(
	parameter int              width = 8,             // word width
	parameter lau_pkg::speed_e speed = lau_pkg::FAST  // performance parameter
) (
	input  logic [width-1:0] GI,  // gen./prop. in
	input  logic [width-1:0] PI,  // gen./prop. in
	output logic [width-1:0] GO,  // gen./prop. out
	output logic [width-1:0] PO   // gen./prop. out
);

	// Constants
	localparam int n = width;  // prefix structure width
	localparam int m = $clog2(width);  // prefix structure depth

	// Sklansky parallel-prefix carry-lookahead structure
	if (speed == lau_pkg::FAST) begin : fastPrefix
		logic [(m+1)*n-1:0] GT, PT;  // gen./prop. temp
			assign GT[n-1:0] = GI;
			assign PT[n-1:0] = PI;
			for (genvar l = 1; l <= m; l++) begin : levels
				for (genvar k = 0; k < 2 ** (m - l); k++) begin : groups
					for (genvar i = 0; i < 2 ** (l - 1); i++) begin : bits
						// pass prop and gen to following nodes
						if ((k * 2 ** l + i) < n) begin : white
							assign GT[l*n+k*2**l+i] = GT[(l-1)*n+k*2**l+i];
							assign PT[l*n+k*2**l+i] = PT[(l-1)*n+k*2**l+i];
						end
						// calculate new propagate and generate
						if ((k * 2 ** l + 2 ** (l - 1) + i) < n) begin : black
							assign GT[l*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) + i] = 
												GT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) + i]
											  | (  PT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) + i]
												 & GT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) - 1] );
							assign PT[l*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) + i] = 
													PT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) + i]
												  & PT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) - 1];
						end
					end
				end
			end
			assign GO = GT[(m+1)*n-1 : m*n];
			assign PO = PT[(m+1)*n-1 : m*n];
	end

	// Brent-Kung parallel-prefix carry-lookahead structure
	if (speed == lau_pkg::MEDIUM) begin : mediumPrefix
		logic [(2*m)*n -1:0] GT, PT;  // gen./prop. temp
		assign GT[n-1:0] = GI;
		assign PT[n-1:0] = PI;

		for (genvar l = 1; l <= m; l++) begin : levels1
			for (genvar k = 0; k < 2**(m-l); k++) begin : groups
				for (genvar i = 0; i < 2**l -1; i++) begin : bits
					if ((k* 2**l +i) < n) begin : white
						assign GT[l*n + k* 2**l +i] = GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**l +i];
						assign PT[l*n + k* 2**l +i] = PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**l +i];
					end // white
				end // bits
				if ((k* 2**l + 2**l -1) < n) begin : black
					assign GT[l*n + k* 2**l + 2**l -1] =
								GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**l + 2**l - 1] |
							  | (  PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**l + 2**l     -1] 
							     & GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**l + 2**(l-1) -1]);
					assign PT[l*n + k* 2**l + 2**l -1] =
								PT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**l     -1] 
							  & PT[(l-1)*n + k*2**l + 2**(l-1) -1];
				end // black
			end
		end // level1
		for (genvar l = m +1; l < 2*m; l++) begin : levels2
			for (genvar i = 0; i < 2**(2*m -l); i++) begin : bits
				if (i < n) begin : white
					assign GT[l*n +i] = GT[(l-1)*n +i];
					assign PT[l*n +i] = PT[(l-1)*n +i];
				end // white
			end // bits
			for (genvar k = 1; k < 2**(l-m); k++) begin : groups
				if (l < 2*m -1) begin : empty
					for (genvar i = 0; i < 2**(2*m -l -1) -1; i++) begin : bits
						if ((k* 2**(2*m -l) +i) < n) begin : white
							assign GT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i] = GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i];
							assign PT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i] = PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i];
						end // white
					end
				end // empty
				if ((k* 2**(2*m -l) + 2**(2*m -l -1) -1) < n) begin : black
					assign GT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) + 2**(2*m -l-1) -1] = 
								GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m-l) + 2**(2*m -l-1) -1]
							  | (  PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m-l) + 2**(2*m-l-1) -1] 
								 & GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m-l) -1] );
					assign PT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) + 2**(2*m -l-1) -1] = 
								PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) + 2**(2*m -l-1) -1]
							  & PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) -1];
				end // black
				for (genvar i = 2**(2*m -l-1); i < 2**(2*m -l); i++) begin : bits
					if ((k* 2**(2*m -l) +i) < n) begin : white
						assign GT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i] = GT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i];
						assign PT[l*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i] = PT[(l-1)*n + k* 2**(2*m -l) +i];
					end // white
				end
			end
		end // level2
		assign GO = GT[2*m*n -1 : (2*m -1) * n];
		assign PO = PT[2*m*n -1 : (2*m -1) * n];
	end  // Serial-prefix carry-lookahead structure
	else if (speed == lau_pkg::SLOW) begin : slowPrefix
		logic [n-1:0] GT, PT;  // gen./prop. temp
		assign GT[0] = GI[0];
		assign PT[0] = PI[0];
		
		for (genvar i = 1; i < n; i++) begin : bits
			assign GT[i] = GI[i] | (PI[i] & GT[i-1]);
			assign PT[i] = PI[i] & PT[i-1];
		end
		assign GO = GT;
		assign PO = PT;
	end

endmodule

```

wire signed [31:0] block_in_0_1 = {{16{inport_data0_i[15]}}, inport_data0_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_2_3 = {{16{inport_data1_i[15]}}, inport_data1_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_4_5 = {{16{inport_data2_i[15]}}, inport_data2_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_6_7 = {{16{inport_data3_i[15]}}, inport_data3_i};

//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// IDCT
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
reg signed [31:0] i0;
reg signed [31:0] mul0_a;
reg signed [31:0] mul0_b;
reg signed [31:0] mul1_a;
reg signed [31:0] mul1_b;
reg signed [31:0] mul2_a;
reg signed [31:0] mul2_b;
reg signed [31:0] mul3_a;
reg signed [31:0] mul3_b;
reg signed [31:0] mul4_a;
reg signed [31:0] mul4_b;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    i0     <= 32'b0;
    mul0_a <= 32'b0;
    mul0_b <= 32'b0;
    mul1_a <= 32'b0;
    mul1_b <= 32'b0;
    mul2_a <= 32'b0;
    mul2_b <= 32'b0;
    mul3_a <= 32'b0;
    mul3_b <= 32'b0;
    mul4_a <= 32'b0;
    mul4_b <= 32'b0;
end
else
begin
    /* verilator lint_off WIDTH */
    case (inport_idx_i)
    3'd0:
    begin
        i0     <= block_in_0_1 + block_in_4_5;
        mul0_a <= block_in_2_3;
        mul0_b <= C2_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7;
        mul1_b <= C6_16;
    end
    3'd1:
    begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_0_1;
        mul0_b <= C1_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7;
        mul1_b <= C7_16;
        mul2_a <= block_in_4_5;
        mul2_b <= C5_16;
        mul3_a <= block_in_2_3;
        mul3_b <= C3_16;
        mul4_a <= i0;
        mul4_b <= C4_16;
    end
    3'd2:
    begin
        i0     <= block_in_0_1 - block_in_4_5;
    end
    3'd3:
    begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_0_1;
        mul0_b <= C7_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7;
        mul1_b <= C1_16;
        mul2_a <= block_in_4_5;
        mul2_b <= C3_16;
        mul3_a <= block_in_2_3;
        mul3_b <= C5_16;
    end
    3'd4:
    begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_0_1;
        mul0_b <= C7_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7;
        mul1_b <= C1_16;
        mul2_a <= block_in_4_5;
        mul2_b <= C3_16;
        mul3_a <= block_in_2_3;
        mul3_b <= C5_16;
    end
    3'd5:
    begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_2_3;
        mul0_b <= C6_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7;
        mul1_b <= C2_16;
        mul4_a <= i0;
        mul4_b <= C4_16;
    end
    default:
        ;
    endcase
    /* verilator lint_on WIDTH */
end

reg signed [31:0] mul0_q;
reg signed [31:0] mul1_q;
reg signed [31:0] mul2_q;
reg signed [31:0] mul3_q;
reg signed [31:0] mul4_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    mul0_q <= 32'b0;
    mul1_q <= 32'b0;
    mul2_q <= 32'b0;
    mul3_q <= 32'b0;
    mul4_q <= 32'b0;
end
else
begin
    mul0_q <= mul0_a * mul0_b;
    mul1_q <= mul1_a * mul1_b;
    mul2_q <= mul2_a * mul2_b;
    mul3_q <= mul3_a * mul3_b;
    mul4_q <= mul4_a * mul4_b;
end

reg signed [31:0] mul0;
reg signed [31:0] mul1;
reg signed [31:0] mul2;
reg signed [31:0] mul3;
reg signed [31:0] mul4;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    mul0 <= 32'b0;
    mul1 <= 32'b0;
    mul2 <= 32'b0;
    mul3 <= 32'b0;
    mul4 <= 32'b0;
end
else
begin
    mul0 <= mul0_q;
    mul1 <= mul1_q;
    mul2 <= mul2_q;
    mul3 <= mul3_q;
    mul4 <= mul4_q;
end

reg        out_stg0_valid_q;
reg [2:0]  out_stg0_idx_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    out_stg0_valid_q <= 1'b0;
    out_stg0_idx_q   <= 3'b0;
end
else
begin
    out_stg0_valid_q <= inport_valid_i;
    out_stg0_idx_q   <= inport_idx_i;
end

reg        out_stg1_valid_q;
reg [2:0]  out_stg1_idx_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    out_stg1_valid_q <= 1'b0;
    out_stg1_idx_q   <= 3'b0;
end
else
begin
    out_stg1_valid_q <= out_stg0_valid_q;
    out_stg1_idx_q   <= out_stg0_idx_q;
end

reg        out_stg2_valid_q;
reg [2:0]  out_stg2_idx_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    out_stg2_valid_q <= 1'b0;
    out_stg2_idx_q   <= 3'b0;
end
else
begin
    out_stg2_valid_q <= out_stg1_valid_q;
    out_stg2_idx_q   <= out_stg1_idx_q;
end

reg signed [31:0] o_s5;
reg signed [31:0] o_s6;
reg signed [31:0] o_s7;
reg signed [31:0] o_t0;
reg signed [31:0] o_t1;
reg signed [31:0] o_t2;
reg signed [31:0] o_t3;
reg signed [31:0] o_t4;
reg signed [31:0] o_t5;
reg signed [31:0] o_t6;
reg signed [31:0] o_t7;
reg signed [31:0] o_t6_5;
reg signed [31:0] o_t5_6;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    o_s5   <= 32'b0;
    o_s6   <= 32'b0;
    o_s7   <= 32'b0;
    o_t0   <= 32'b0;
    o_t1   <= 32'b0;
    o_t2   <= 32'b0;
    o_t3   <= 32'b0;
    o_t4   <= 32'b0;
    o_t5   <= 32'b0;
    o_t6   <= 32'b0;
    o_t7   <= 32'b0;
    o_t6_5 <= 32'b0;
    o_t5_6 <= 32'b0;
end
else
begin
    case (out_stg2_idx_q)
    3'd0:
    begin
        o_t3 <= mul0 + mul1; // s3
    end
    3'd1:
    begin
        o_s7 <= mul0 + mul1;
        o_s6 <= mul2 + mul3;
        o_t0 <= mul4;        // s0
    end
    3'd2:
    begin
        o_t0 <= o_t0 + o_t3; // t0
        o_t3 <= o_t0 - o_t3; // t3
        o_t7 <= o_s6 + o_s7;
    end
    3'd3:
    begin
        o_t4 <= (mul0 - mul1) + (mul2 - mul3);
    end
    3'd4:
    begin
        o_t0 <= mul0 - mul1; // s4
        o_s5 <= mul2 - mul3;    
    end
    3'd5:
    begin
        o_t3 <= mul0 - mul1; // s2
        o_t4 <= mul4; // s1
        o_t5 <= o_t0 - o_s5;
        o_t6 <= o_s7 - o_s6;
    end
    3'd6:
    begin
        o_t1 <= o_t4 + o_t3;
        o_t2 <= o_t4 - o_t3;
        o_t6_5 <= o_t6 - o_t5;
        o_t5_6 <= o_t5 + o_t6;
    end
    default:
    begin
        o_s5 <= (o_t6_5 * 181) / 256; // 1/sqrt(2)
        o_s6 <= (o_t5_6 * 181) / 256; // 1/sqrt(2)
    end
    endcase
end

reg        out_stg3_valid_q;
reg [2:0]  out_stg3_idx_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    out_stg3_valid_q <= 1'b0;
    out_stg3_idx_q   <= 3'b0;
end
else
begin
    out_stg3_valid_q <= out_stg2_valid_q;
    out_stg3_idx_q   <= out_stg2_idx_q;
end

reg signed [31:0] block_out[0:7];
reg signed [31:0] block_out_tmp;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    block_out[0] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[1] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[2] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[3] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[4] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[5] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[6] <= 32'b0;
    block_out[7] <= 32'b0;
    block_out_tmp <= 32'b0;
end
else if (out_stg3_valid_q)
begin
    if (out_stg3_idx_q == 3'd3)
    begin
        block_out[0] <= ((o_t0 + o_t7) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
        block_out_tmp <= ((o_t0 - o_t7) >>> OUT_SHIFT); // block_out[7]
        block_out[3] <= ((o_t3 + o_t4) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
        block_out[4] <= ((o_t3 - o_t4) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
    end

    if (out_stg3_idx_q == 3'd6)
        block_out[7] <= block_out_tmp;

    if (out_stg3_idx_q == 3'd7)
    begin
        block_out[2] <= ((o_t2 + o_s5) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
        block_out[5] <= ((o_t2 - o_s5) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
        block_out[1] <= ((o_t1 + o_s6) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
        block_out[6] <= ((o_t1 - o_s6) >>> OUT_SHIFT);
    end
end

reg [7:0] valid_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
    valid_q  <= 8'b0;
else if (img_start_i)
    valid_q  <= 8'b0;
else
    valid_q <= {valid_q[6:0], out_stg3_valid_q};

reg [5:0] ptr_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
    ptr_q <= 6'd0;
else if (img_start_i)
    ptr_q <= 6'd0;
else if (outport_valid_o)
    ptr_q <= ptr_q + 6'd1;

assign outport_valid_o = valid_q[6];
assign outport_data_o  = block_out[ptr_q[2:0]];

assign outport_idx_o   = ptr_q;



endmodule

[Step 4/60]

module jpeg_idct_transpose
(
    // Inputs
     input           clk
    ,input           rst
    ,input           img_start_i
    ,input           img_end_i
    ,input           inport_valid_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  inport_data_i
    ,input  [  5:0]  inport_idx_i
    ,input           outport_ready_i

    // Outputs
    ,output          inport_accept_o
    ,output          outport_valid_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  outport_data0_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  outport_data1_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  outport_data2_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  outport_data3_o
    ,output [  2:0]  outport_idx_o
);



reg         block_wr_q;
reg         block_rd_q;
reg [5:0]   rd_idx_q;
reg [3:0]   rd_addr_q;

wire [4:0]  wr_ptr_w = {block_wr_q, inport_idx_i[3:0]};

wire [31:0] outport_data0_w;
wire [31:0] outport_data1_w;
wire [31:0] outport_data2_w;
wire [31:0] outport_data3_w;

wire wr0_w = inport_valid_i && inport_accept_o && (inport_idx_i[5:4] == 2'd0);
wire wr1_w = inport_valid_i && inport_accept_o && (inport_idx_i[5:4] == 2'd1);
wire wr2_w = inport_valid_i && inport_accept_o && (inport_idx_i[5:4] == 2'd2);
wire wr3_w = inport_valid_i && inport_accept_o && (inport_idx_i[5:4] == 2'd3);

jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
u_ram0
(
     .clk0_i(clk)
    ,.rst0_i(rst)
    ,.clk1_i(clk)
    ,.rst1_i(rst)

    ,.addr0_i(wr_ptr_w)
    ,.data0_i(inport_data_i)
    ,.wr0_i(wr0_w)
    ,.data0_o()

    ,.addr1_i({block_rd_q, rd_addr_q})
    ,.data1_i(32'b0)
    ,.wr1_i(1'b0)
    ,.data1_o(outport_data0_w)
);

jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
u_ram1
(
     .clk0_i(clk)
    ,.rst0_i(rst)
    ,.clk1_i(clk)
    ,.rst1_i(rst)

    ,.addr0_i(wr_ptr_w)
    ,.data0_i(inport_data_i)
    ,.wr0_i(wr1_w)
    ,.data0_o()

    ,.addr1_i({block_rd_q, rd_addr_q})
    ,.data1_i(32'b0)
    ,.wr1_i(1'b0)
    ,.data1_o(outport_data1_w)
);

jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
u_ram2
(
     .clk0_i(clk)
    ,.rst0_i(rst)
    ,.clk1_i(clk)
    ,.rst1_i(rst)

    ,.addr0_i(wr_ptr_w)
    ,.data0_i(inport_data_i)
    ,.wr0_i(wr2_w)
    ,.data0_o()

    ,.addr1_i({block_rd_q, rd_addr_q})
    ,.data1_i(32'b0)
    ,.wr1_i(1'b0)
    ,.data1_o(outport_data2_w)
);

jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
u_ram3
(
     .clk0_i(clk)
    ,.rst0_i(rst)
    ,.clk1_i(clk)
    ,.rst1_i(rst)

    ,.addr0_i(wr_ptr_w)
    ,.data0_i(inport_data_i)
    ,.wr0_i(wr3_w)
    ,.data0_o()

    ,.addr1_i({block_rd_q, rd_addr_q})
    ,.data1_i(32'b0)
    ,.wr1_i(1'b0)
    ,.data1_o(outport_data3_w)
);

//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// Input Buffer
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
reg [1:0] block_ready_q;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
begin
    block_ready_q     <= 2'b0;
    block_wr_q        <= 1'b0;
    block_rd_q        <= 1'b0;
end
else if (img_start_i)
begin
    block_ready_q     <= 2'b0;
    block_wr_q        <= 1'b0;
    block_rd_q        <= 1'b0;
end
else
begin
    if (inport_valid_i && inport_idx_i == 6'd63 && inport_accept_o)
    begin
        block_ready_q[block_wr_q] <= 1'b1;
        block_wr_q                <= ~block_wr_q;
    end

    if (outport_valid_o && rd_idx_q[5:0] == 6'd63)
    begin
        block_ready_q[block_rd_q] <= 1'b0;
        block_rd_q <= ~block_rd_q;
    end
end

assign inport_accept_o = ~block_ready_q[block_wr_q];

//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// FSM
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
localparam STATE_W           = 2;
localparam STATE_IDLE        = 2'd0;
localparam STATE_SETUP       = 2'd1;
localparam STATE_ACTIVE      = 2'd2;

reg [STATE_W-1:0] state_q;
reg [STATE_W-1:0] next_state_r;

always @ *
begin
    next_state_r = state_q;

    case (state_q)
    STATE_IDLE:
    begin
        if (block_ready_q[block_rd_q] && outport_ready_i)
            next_state_r = STATE_SETUP;
    end
    STATE_SETUP:
    begin
        next_state_r = STATE_ACTIVE;
    end
    STATE_ACTIVE:
    begin
        if (outport_valid_o && rd_idx_q == 6'd63)
            next_state_r = STATE_IDLE;
    end
    default: ;
    endcase

    if (img_start_i)
        next_state_r = STATE_IDLE;
end

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
    state_q <= STATE_IDLE;
else
    state_q <= next_state_r;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
    rd_idx_q <= 6'b0;
else if (img_start_i)
    rd_idx_q <= 6'b0;
else if (state_q == STATE_ACTIVE)
    rd_idx_q <= rd_idx_q + 6'd1;

always @ (posedge clk )
if (rst)
    rd_addr_q <= 4'b0;
else if (state_q == STATE_IDLE)
    rd_addr_q <= 4'b0;
else if (state_q == STATE_SETUP)
    rd_addr_q <= 4'd8;
else if (state_q == STATE_ACTIVE)
begin
    case (rd_idx_q[2:0])
    3'd0: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q - 4'd8;
    3'd1: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q + 4'd8;
    3'd2: ;
    3'd3: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q - 4'd8;
    3'd4: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q + 4'd8;
    3'd5: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q - 4'd8;
    3'd6: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q + 4'd1;
    3'd7: rd_addr_q <= rd_addr_q + 4'd8;
    endcase
end

assign outport_valid_o = (state_q == STATE_ACTIVE);
assign outport_idx_o   = rd_idx_q[2:0];
assign outport_data0_o = outport_data0_w;
assign outport_data1_o = outport_data1_w;
assign outport_data2_o = outport_data2_w;
assign outport_data3_o = outport_data3_w;


endmodule

module jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
(
    // Inputs
     input           clk0_i
    ,input           rst0_i
    ,input  [  4:0]  addr0_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  data0_i
    ,input           wr0_i
    ,input           clk1_i
    ,input           rst1_i
    ,input  [  4:0]  addr1_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  data1_i
    ,input           wr1_i

    // Outputs
    ,output [ 31:0]  data0_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  data1_o
);



//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// Dual Port RAM
// Mode: Read First
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
/* verilator lint_off MULTIDRIVEN */
reg [31:0]   ram [31:0] /*verilator public*/;
/* verilator lint_on MULTIDRIVEN */

reg [31:0] ram_read0_q;
reg [31:0] ram_read1_q;


// Synchronous write
always @ (posedge clk0_i)
begin
    if (wr0_i)
        ram[addr0_i][31:0] <= data0_i[31:0];

    ram_read0_q <= ram[addr0_i];
end

always @ (posedge clk1_i)
begin
    if (wr1_i)
        ram[addr1_i][31:0] <= data1_i[31:0];

    ram_read1_q <= ram[addr1_i];
end


assign data0_o = ram_read0_q;
assign data1_o = ram_read1_q;



endmodule

module jpeg_idct_y
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// Params
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
#(
     parameter OUT_SHIFT        = 15
    ,parameter INPUT_WIDTH      = 32
)
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
// Ports
//-----------------------------------------------------------------
(
    // Inputs
     input           clk
    ,input           rst
    ,input           img_start_i
    ,input           img_end_i
    ,input           inport_valid_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  inport_data0_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  inport_data1_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  inport_data2_i
    ,input  [ 31:0]  inport_data3_i
    ,input  [  2:0]  inport_idx_i

    // Outputs
    ,output          outport_valid_o
    ,output [ 31:0]  outport_data_o
    ,output [  5:0]  outport_idx_o
);

Actually, let me think about the transpose more carefully. The transpose stores 64 values per block, double-buffered = 128 entries. The 4 RAMs each hold 32 entries (64 entries / 2 blocks, but spread across 4 RAMs). Each RAM is 32 entries × 32 bits. The read mux is 32:1.

If I split each 32-entry RAM into two 16-entry RAMs (using `rd_addr_q[3]` to select between them), I can reduce the mux from 32:1 to 16:1. But that doubles the number of RAMs. Not great for area.

Better approach: **Add a pipeline register to the read address**. The transpose already has a setup→active state machine with 1 cycle of setup. If I register the address earlier, I can break the critical path. Let me look at the timing more carefully.

The critical path is: `rd_addr_q` (register) → 32:1 mux → `ram_read1_q` (register). This is one clock cycle. The 1707 ps mux delay dominates. 

What if I change the transpose to use **asynchronous reads** (combinational read) followed by a pipeline register? That would move the mux to combinational logic but the path would still be the same register→mux→register.

Actually the fundamental problem is the 32-deep register array. Let me try a different approach: **reduce the number of entries per RAM**. The transpose reads 4 values per cycle (4 lanes). If I use 8 RAMs instead of 4 (16 entries each), the mux becomes 16:1 which is faster. But the total FFs stay the same and area increases.

Let me try a completely different strategy: since the critical path is in the transpose RAM read, let me try to **pipeline the read** by adding an extra register stage. The transpose currently outputs data 1 cycle after the read address. If I make it 2 cycles, the mux has 2 clock periods to settle... no, that's not how it works. The mux settles within one clock period. Adding a pipeline register after the mux doesn't help because the mux output feeds a register already.

Looking at the schedule: the multiplier operands are set up based on `inport_idx_i`, and the results are used 2 cycles later based on `out_stg2_idx_q`. The `mul_q` is the multiplier output registered, and `mul` is another register stage. These are both needed because the schedule uses mul results at specific pipeline depths.

Let me try a major restructuring: **merge the two IDCT passes into a more optimized form**. But that's complex. Let me first try simpler optimizations.

Let me try removing the unnecessary `mul_q` → `mul` double-registration by checking if the pipeline depth allows it. The timing is:
- Cycle 0: inport_idx_i sets mul_a/mul_b based on idx
- Cycle 1: mul_q = mul_a * mul_b (multiplier result registered)
- Cycle 2: mul = mul_q (pipeline register)
- Cycle 3: out_stg2_idx_q = inport_idx_i (3 cycles delayed), results used

So mul is used at cycle 3 (out_stg2 = cycle 0 idx + 3 cycles delay). And mul_q is at cycle 2. The computation stage uses `mul` at `out_stg2_idx_q`. If I remove the `mul` register, I'd use `mul_q` at `out_stg2_idx_q` which would be cycle 2 but out_stg2 is cycle 3. So I'd need to adjust the pipeline delay.

Actually, wait. Let me recount:
- `inport_idx_i` → `out_stg0_idx_q` (1 cycle)
- `out_stg0_idx_q` → `out_stg1_idx_q` (1 cycle)  
- `out_stg1_idx_q` → `out_stg2_idx_q` (1 cycle)
- `out_stg2_idx_q` → `out_stg3_idx_q` (1 cycle)

So `out_stg2_idx_q` is `inport_idx_i` delayed by 3 cycles. And `mul` is `mul_a*mul_b` delayed by 2 cycles (mul_q + mul). So at cycle 3 (out_stg2), the mul results correspond to cycle 0's operands, but with only 2 cycles of delay. This means the mul results are ready 1 cycle before they're used. The `mul` register adds 1 cycle to align.

If I remove `mul` and use `mul_q` directly, the mul results arrive at cycle 2 but are used at cycle 3 (out_stg2). That's 1 cycle too early - the data would be stale by the time out_stg2 fires. No, actually the registers mul_q and mul are just pipeline delays - they hold the value. At cycle 3, `mul` holds the value computed at cycle 1 (from cycle 0's operands). `mul_q` at cycle 3 holds the value computed at cycle 2 (from cycle 1's operands). So removing `mul` would mean at cycle 3 we'd be using cycle 1's operands instead of cycle 0's. That breaks the schedule.

So the `mul` register is necessary for correct alignment. Let me try other optimizations.

Let me try **reducing the transpose RAM size** by not double-buffering. The spec says "the stream of a block overlaps the next block's input beats (the transpose double-buffers one full block)." So double-buffering is required for correctness. But maybe I can restructure to reduce mux depth.

[Step 11/60]
// Self-checking testbench for jpeg_idct_2d — the full 8x8 2-D IDCT
// (x-pass -> transpose -> y-pass) of ultraembedded/core_jpeg.
// Reads vectors.dat: one line per 8x8 block = 64 input coefficients (16-bit
// signed, 4 hex digits, row-major c[row][col]) followed by 64 expected outputs
// (32-bit, 8 hex digits, in ROW-MAJOR pixel order — outputs are matched via
// outport_idx_o, which labels each streamed value with its row-major position).
//
// Drive protocol: identical to the jpeg_idct_x benchmark (the upstream block
// serializer's fixed schedule): per row 8 back-to-back valid beats idx=0..7,
// EVEN coefficient set (c0,c2,c4,c6) at idx in {0,2,5,7}, ODD set (c1,c3,c5,c7)
// at idx in {1,3,4,6}; 8 rows back-to-back per 64-beat block; >= 2 idle cycles
// between blocks (valid=0, idx=0, data=0).
//
// Output contract (fixed schedule): 64 values per block streamed on
// outport_valid_o; the k-th value of a block carries
// outport_idx_o == {k[2:0], k[5:3]} (the y-pass un-transpose permutation) and
// must equal the expected output at that row-major position. Comparison is
// EXACT (integer outputs).
module tb;
  int total_checks;
  int total_errors;

  logic        clk;
  logic        rst;
  logic        img_start;
  logic        inport_valid;
  logic [15:0] inport_data0, inport_data1, inport_data2, inport_data3;
  logic [2:0]  inport_idx;
  logic        outport_valid;
  logic [31:0] outport_data;
  logic [5:0]  outport_idx;

    .img_start_i(img_start), .img_end_i(1'b0),

  always #5 clk = ~clk;

  // ---- load vectors.dat: flat token stream, 128 tokens per block ----------

[ASSISTANT]
  -> read_file(filename='tb.sv', limit=120, offset=50)

[TOOL RESULT]
[Step 12/60]
  logic [15:0] coeffs [0:MAX_BLOCKS*64-1];
  logic [31:0] expy   [0:MAX_BLOCKS*64-1];
  int num_blocks;

  task automatic load_vectors;
    int fd, rc, n;
    logic [31:0] tok;
    logic [31:0] flat [0:MAX_BLOCKS*128-1];
    fd = $fopen("vectors.dat", "r");
    if (fd == 0) begin
      $display("ERROR: cannot open vectors.dat");
      $fatal(1);
    end
    n = 0;
    while (!$feof(fd)) begin
      rc = $fscanf(fd, "%h", tok);
      if (rc == 1) begin
        flat[n] = tok;
        n = n + 1;
      end
    end
    $fclose(fd);
    num_blocks = n / 128;
    if (num_blocks * 128 != n)
      $display("WARNING: %0d trailing tokens ignored", n - num_blocks * 128);
    for (int b = 0; b < num_blocks; b++)
      for (int i = 0; i < 64; i++) begin
        coeffs[b*64+i] = flat[b*128+i][15:0];
        expy[b*64+i]   = flat[b*128+64+i];
      end
  endtask

  // ---- output checker -------------------------------------------------------
  int out_cnt;
  bit checking;
  logic [5:0] k;

  always @(posedge clk) begin
    #1;
    if (checking && outport_valid) begin
      total_checks = total_checks + 1;
      k = out_cnt % 64;
      if (outport_idx !== {k[2:0], k[5:3]}) begin
        $display("TB_ERROR block=%0d out=%0d idx=%0d expected_idx=%0d",
                 out_cnt / 64, out_cnt % 64, outport_idx, {k[2:0], k[5:3]});
        total_errors = total_errors + 1;
      end
      if (outport_data !== expy[(out_cnt / 64) * 64 + outport_idx]) begin
        $display("TB_ERROR block=%0d pos=%0d expected_y=%08x actual_y=%08x",
                 out_cnt / 64, outport_idx, expy[(out_cnt / 64) * 64 + outport_idx],
                 outport_data);
        total_errors = total_errors + 1;
      end
`ifdef DUMP_Y
      $display("Y %02x %08x", outport_idx, outport_data);
`endif
      out_cnt = out_cnt + 1;
    end
  end

  // ---- driver ----------------------------------------------------------------
  task automatic drive_beat(input logic valid, input logic [2:0] kk,
                            input logic [15:0] d0, d1, d2, d3);
    @(negedge clk);
    inport_valid = valid;
    inport_idx   = kk;
    inport_data0 = d0;
    inport_data1 = d1;
    inport_data2 = d2;
    inport_data3 = d3;
  endtask

  initial begin
    total_checks = 0;
    total_errors = 0;
    out_cnt = 0;
    checking = 0;
    rst = 1;
    img_start = 0;
    inport_valid = 0;
    inport_idx = 0;
    inport_data0 = 0; inport_data1 = 0; inport_data2 = 0; inport_data3 = 0;

    load_vectors();

    repeat (4) @(negedge clk);
    rst = 0;
    @(negedge clk);
    img_start = 1;             // one-cycle image-start pulse
    @(negedge clk);
    img_start = 0;
    checking = 1;

    for (int b = 0; b < num_blocks; b++) begin
      for (int r = 0; r < 8; r++) begin
        for (int kb = 0; kb < 8; kb++) begin
          if (kb == 0 || kb == 2 || kb == 5 || kb == 7)
            drive_beat(1'b1, kb[2:0], coeffs[b*64+r*8+0], coeffs[b*64+r*8+2],
                                      coeffs[b*64+r*8+4], coeffs[b*64+r*8+6]);
          else
            drive_beat(1'b1, kb[2:0], coeffs[b*64+r*8+1], coeffs[b*64+r*8+3],
                                      coeffs[b*64+r*8+5], coeffs[b*64+r*8+7]);
        end
      end
      // inter-block gap: >= 2 idle cycles (serializer IDLE+SETUP), varied a bit
      repeat (2 + (b % 3)) drive_beat(1'b0, 3'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0);
    end
    drive_beat(1'b0, 3'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0, 16'd0);
    repeat (400) @(negedge clk);  // flush x + transpose + y pipeline

    if (out_cnt != num_blocks * 64) begin
      $display("TB_ERROR missing outputs: got %0d, expected %0d", out_cnt, num_blocks * 64);
      total_errors = total_errors + 1;
    end
    $display("TB_SUMMARY total=%0d errors=%0d", total_checks, total_errors);
    if (total_errors != 0) $fatal(1, "FAIL");
    $display("PASS");
    $finish;
  end
endmodule

... (showing lines 50-169 of 169; use offset/limit for more)

[ASSISTANT]
Now I understand the testbench fully. The key insight is that `outport_idx_o` must be `{k[2:0], k[5:3]}` where k is the output count within a block. The y-pass already computes this.

Now let me think about the main optimization strategy. The critical path is the transpose RAM read mux (32:1). Let me try to break it by adding a pipeline register in the read path. 

The transpose has:
- `rd_addr_q` → RAM read (synchronous, 1 cycle) → `ram_read1_q` → feeds y-pass

If I add an extra pipeline register after the RAM read, I get 2 cycles of read latency, but the mux still takes 1 clock period. The issue is the mux path is `rd_addr_q[4:0]` → 32:1 mux → register, all within one clock. The mux is 1707 ps.

The fix: **register the read address one cycle before the RAM read** so the mux path starts from a clean register. But that's already the case - `rd_addr_q` IS a register.

Actually, the 1707 ps includes the Q→output delay of the register PLUS the mux. The real issue is that 32:1 mux is inherently slow. Let me try to split the RAM into smaller banks.

Let me try a different approach: **reduce the transpose to use 16-entry RAMs** by changing the addressing. Currently each RAM has 32 entries (5-bit address = block select bit + 4-bit index). If I use the block select as a separate mux outside the RAM, each RAM only needs 16 entries (4-bit address), and I select between the two blocks' read outputs with a 2:1 mux.

Let me create an optimized version:

Good, back to 8.98M. Now let me think about more significant structural changes.

The critical path is `rd_addr_q` (Q→output 1400 ps) → mux decode → register. The 1400 ps is the 32:1 mux. This is the inherent cost of a 32-entry register array read.

**Key idea**: What if I add a pipeline register to break the 32:1 mux into two 16:1 stages? I can add a register after the first 16:1 mux level. This would halve the critical path through the mux but add 1 cycle of read latency. The transpose has a SETUP state that provides 1 cycle of margin, so I might be able to absorb this.

But wait - the issue is that the 32:1 mux is within a single clock cycle (register → mux → register). Adding a pipeline register in the middle of the mux would require restructuring the RAM read. Yosys handles this automatically if I add a register stage.

Actually, let me try a different approach: **pipeline the read address computation**. The `rd_addr_q` is computed with adders/subtractors that feed the RAM read. If I precompute `rd_addr_q` one cycle earlier (register it), the RAM read path starts from a clean register. But `rd_addr_q` IS already a register - the issue is the 32:1 mux after it.

Let me try yet another approach: **register the RAM read output one more time**. Currently: `rd_addr_q` → 32:1 mux → `ram_read1_q` (registered). If I split into: `rd_addr_q` → 16:1 mux → `intermediate_q` (registered) → 16:1 mux → `ram_read1_q` (registered). This breaks the mux into 2 cycles. But I'd need the read to take 2 cycles instead of 1, which means the transpose needs to start reading 1 cycle earlier.

This is getting complex. Let me try a completely different strategy: **eliminate the transpose entirely** by restructuring the data flow.

The transpose does a corner-turn: the x-pass outputs row-major, and the y-pass needs column-major input. What if I restructure the x-pass to write directly to a buffer that the y-pass reads in column-major order, without the separate transpose module?

Actually, the transpose IS that buffer. The 4 RAMs are the corner-turn buffer. Let me think about whether I can reduce its size or mux depth.

The transpose stores 64 values per block (double-buffered). Each value is 32 bits. The 4 RAMs store 32 values each (2 blocks × 16 positions). The read accesses 4 RAMs simultaneously (4 values per cycle). 

What if I use a single 64-entry buffer (not double-buffered) and accept that I can't overlap input and output? The spec says output overlaps the next block's input. But the testbench has gaps between blocks (2+ idle cycles). Let me check if a single buffer would work:

Looking at the testbench: 64 input beats per block, then 2-4 idle cycles, then next block. The x-pass has ~7 cycle pipeline latency. The transpose starts outputting after a block is fully written. With a single buffer, I'd need the output to finish before the next block's write starts. The output is 64 beats. The input for the next block starts 2-4 cycles after the previous block's last input. So the output would need to start almost immediately and finish within ~64+4 = 68 cycles. That's tight but might work if the output starts right after the block is complete.

Actually, with a single buffer I'd need: the block is fully written at cycle 64 (from block start), then the output starts and takes 64 cycles. The next block's input starts at cycle 64 + 2-4 = 66-68. The output would finish at cycle 64 + 64 = 128. But the next block starts writing at cycle 66-68, which would overwrite the buffer before output is done. So single buffering doesn't work.

Let me try a different approach to reduce the mux: **use 8 RAMs of 16 entries instead of 4 of 32**. The mux becomes 16:1. I need to read 4 values per cycle, and each value could come from a different RAM depending on the access pattern.

Let me study the transpose write/read patterns more carefully:

**Write pattern**: The x-pass outputs values with idx 0..63. The transpose writes to `ram[{block_wr_q, idx[3:0]}]` in RAM `idx[5:4]`. So:
- RAM 0: positions 0-15 (idx 0-15, i.e., row 0-1)
- RAM 1: positions 0-15 (idx 16-31, i.e., row 2-3)
- RAM 2: positions 0-15 (idx 32-47, i.e., row 4-5)
- RAM 3: positions 0-15 (idx 48-63, i.e., row 6-7)

Each RAM stores 16 values per block, 32 total (double-buffered).

**Read pattern**: The read address `rd_addr_q` generates a specific access pattern. All 4 RAMs are read simultaneously, giving 4 values. The `rd_idx_q` counts 0..63, and `rd_addr_q` follows a specific +/-8, +/-1 pattern.

The read pattern accesses the transpose in column-major order: for each column, it reads 4 values at a time (2 rows × 2 values per row, or similar). The `rd_addr_q` pattern ensures the right elements are read.

This is complex. Let me try a simpler optimization: **reduce the bit width of the transpose RAM**. The x-pass output is `>>> 11` (arithmetic right shift by 11), giving a 32-bit result. Actually, let me look at this from a different angle. The area is 4264 and delay is 2105. Cost = 4264 × 2105 = 8.97M. To reduce cost, I need to reduce either area or delay. The delay is dominated by the RAM mux. The area is dominated by the RAM FFs.

Actually, let me try a bold idea: **merge the transpose and y-pass**. This is essentially what the transpose does, but maybe I can integrate it more tightly. Let me think...

The x-pass processes 8 rows, each producing 8 output values. The y-pass needs all 8 values of a column (one from each row) to process a column. So the y-pass needs to wait until all 8 rows are processed before starting.

The transpose buffer holds the entire 8×8 block of x-pass results. The y-pass reads them in column order. This is fundamentally a 64-entry buffer.

Can I reduce this to fewer entries? The y-pass processes 8 beats per column (idx 0..7), reading 4 values per beat. So it reads 32 values per column? No, it reads 4 values per beat × 8 beats = 32 values per column × 8 columns = 256. Wait, that's too many. Let me re-examine.

Actually, the y-pass receives 4 data lanes per beat and processes 8 beats per column (idx 0..7). 4 lanes × 8 beats = 32 values per column group. With 8 columns, that's 256. But the block only has 64 values. So the transpose must be providing 4 values per beat × 64 beats = 256. Wait, the transpose outputs 64 beats of 4 values each = 256 values? Let me re-examine. The transpose reads 4 RAMs simultaneously, giving 4 values per beat, over 64 beats = 256 values. But the y-pass only needs 64 values per block. So there's a 4× redundancy? No, the y-pass processes the 4 values as `data0..3` which are the even/odd coefficient split. So each beat of the y-pass receives 4 of the 8 values needed for one column's IDCT.

OK so the transpose provides 64 beats × 4 values = 256 data items, but the y-pass only uses 4 per beat. The y-pass processes 8 columns, each in 8 beats, receiving 4 values per beat. Actually, looking at the transpose read pattern: the `rd_addr_q` changes in a specific pattern that reads the same RAM entries multiple times with different addresses. The transpose is reading the 64 stored values in a specific order that provides the right 4 values per beat for the y-pass.

This is getting complicated. Let me try a different, simpler optimization: **try different target_delay values to find a better area-delay trade-off**.

Let me also try reducing the number of pipeline registers in the output valid chain. The `valid_q` is 8 bits, and `outport_valid_o = valid_q[6]`. This is a shift register. Can I reduce it?

The valid pipeline: `out_stg3_valid_q` → valid_q[0] → ... → valid_q[6] (7 stages). Plus `out_stg3` is 4 stages from input. So total 4+7 = 11 stages from input to output valid. The x-pass has 8 beats per row × 8 rows = 64 beats, but the pipeline latency is ~11 cycles. The output starts after the block is fully processed + pipeline delay.

Can I reduce the valid pipeline? The `block_out` writes happen at `out_stg3` (4 stages from input). The data is available at `block_out` and streamed via `ptr_q`. The valid signal needs to assert when the first block_out entry is ready and stream for 8 cycles. The `valid_q` shift register delays the valid by 7 stages from `out_stg3_valid_q`. Why 7 stages?

Looking at the x-pass: `out_stg3_valid_q` asserts when idx=3 is processed (the first block_out writes). But the streaming starts 7 cycles later. This is because the block_out writes happen at specific idx cycles (3, 6, 7) and the streaming needs to wait until all 8 entries are written. The last write is at idx=7, which is `out_stg3` = 4+7 = 11 cycles from block start. Then the streaming starts.

Actually, the `valid_q` is 8 bits and `outport_valid_o = valid_q[6]`. So it's `out_stg3_valid_q` delayed by 7 stages. This means the first output valid is 7 cycles after `out_stg3_valid_q` first asserts (at idx=0). The block_out writes at idx=3,6,7 need to complete before streaming. idx=7 at out_stg3 is 4+7=11 cycles from block start. The valid at `valid_q[6]` is 4+7=11 cycles from block start. So the streaming starts right when the last block_out write happens. That makes sense.

Can I reduce the valid_q from 8 bits to fewer? The issue is that the last block_out write (idx=7) and the first output (ptr_q=0) happen at the same cycle. The ptr_q starts counting when `outport_valid_o` is high. If I reduce the delay, the output would start before all block_out entries are written. So the 7-stage delay is necessary.

Let me try a different approach: **combine the mul_q and mul pipeline stages into one**. Currently:
1. `mul_a/mul_b` set (cycle 0, based on inport_idx_i)
2. `mul_q = mul_a * mul_b` (cycle 1)
3. `mul = mul_q` (cycle 2)
4. Used at `out_stg2_idx_q` (cycle 3, based on 3-stage idx pipeline)

The `mul` register (step 3) just delays mul_q by 1 cycle. What if instead of having `mul` as a separate register, I use `mul_q` directly and adjust the idx pipeline to be 2 stages instead of 3? Then:
1. `mul_a/mul_b` set (cycle 0)
2. `mul_q = mul_a * mul_b` (cycle 1)
3. Used at `out_stg2_idx_q` which is now 2-stage (cycle 2)

But wait, at cycle 2, `mul_q` holds cycle-1's result (from cycle-0 operands). `out_stg2_idx_q` at 2-stage would be cycle-0's idx. So cycle-0's idx aligns with cycle-0's operands (via mul_q at cycle 1, which is cycle 0's result). That's aligned!

But I also need `out_stg3_idx_q` at 3 stages for the block_out writes. So I'd have:
- out_stg1 (1 stage)
- out_stg2 (2 stages) - uses mul_q
- out_stg3 (3 stages) - for block_out writes

This removes the `mul` register (5×32 = 160 FFs) and one idx/valid pipeline stage (1+3 = 4 FFs) per engine, saving ~164 FFs × 2 engines = 328 FFs. That's about 5% of area. Let me try this.

module jpeg_idct_x
#(parameter OUT_SHIFT = 11, parameter INPUT_WIDTH = 16)
(
    input           clk, input           rst,
    input           img_start_i, input           img_end_i,
    input           inport_valid_i,
    input  [ 15:0]  inport_data0_i, input  [ 15:0]  inport_data1_i,
    input  [ 15:0]  inport_data2_i, input  [ 15:0]  inport_data3_i,
    input  [  2:0]  inport_idx_i,
    output          outport_valid_o,
    output [ 31:0]  outport_data_o,
    output [  5:0]  outport_idx_o
);

localparam [15:0] C1_16 = 4017;
localparam [15:0] C2_16 = 3784;
localparam [15:0] C3_16 = 3406;
localparam [15:0] C4_16 = 2896;
localparam [15:0] C5_16 = 2276;
localparam [15:0] C6_16 = 1567;
localparam [15:0] C7_16 = 799;

wire signed [31:0] block_in_0_1 = {{16{inport_data0_i[15]}}, inport_data0_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_2_3 = {{16{inport_data1_i[15]}}, inport_data1_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_4_5 = {{16{inport_data2_i[15]}}, inport_data2_i};
wire signed [31:0] block_in_6_7 = {{16{inport_data3_i[15]}}, inport_data3_i};

reg signed [31:0] i0;
reg signed [31:0] mul0_a, mul0_b, mul1_a, mul1_b, mul2_a, mul2_b, mul3_a, mul3_b, mul4_a, mul4_b;

always @ (posedge clk)
if (rst) begin
    i0 <= 0;
    mul0_a <= 0; mul0_b <= 0; mul1_a <= 0; mul1_b <= 0;
    mul2_a <= 0; mul2_b <= 0; mul3_a <= 0; mul3_b <= 0;
    mul4_a <= 0; mul4_b <= 0;
end
else begin
    case (inport_idx_i)
    3'd0: begin
        i0 <= block_in_0_1 + block_in_4_5;
        mul0_a <= block_in_2_3; mul0_b <= C2_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7; mul1_b <= C6_16;
    end
    3'd1: begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_0_1; mul0_b <= C1_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7; mul1_b <= C7_16;
        mul2_a <= block_in_4_5; mul2_b <= C5_16;
        mul3_a <= block_in_2_3; mul3_b <= C3_16;
        mul4_a <= i0;           mul4_b <= C4_16;
    end
    3'd2: begin
        i0 <= block_in_0_1 - block_in_4_5;
    end
    3'd3: begin

[ASSISTANT]
Let me create design_v3 by modifying design_v2 to remove the `mul` register stage and adjust the idx pipeline. I'll use apply_diff to make targeted changes to both IDCT modules. First, let me understand the exact structure: the pattern `mul0 <= mul0_q` needs to become just using `mul0_q` directly, and the case statement `case (out_stg2_idx_q)` needs to change to use `out_stg1_idx_q` (since I'm removing one stage from the idx pipeline).

Wait, actually this is tricky. Let me reconsider. In the original:
- idx pipeline: stg0 (1c) → stg1 (2c) → stg2 (3c) → stg3 (4c)
- mul pipeline: mul_a/b (0c) → mul_q (1c) → mul (2c)

The case uses `out_stg2_idx_q` (3c) and `mul` (2c from input idx, since mul_a/b is set same cycle as inport_idx_i). So mul is 3c from inport_idx_i (mul_a/b at 0c, mul_q at 1c, mul at 2c... wait, that's 2c). 

Hmm, let me be very precise:
- At cycle T: `inport_idx_i = N`, the always block sets `mul0_a/b` based on `inport_idx_i` at the **next** posedge.
- At cycle T+1: `mul0_a/b` registers are updated (they're set in the same always block). `mul0_q = mul0_a * mul0_b`. `out_stg0_idx_q = N`.
- At cycle T+2: `mul0 = mul0_q`. `out_stg1_idx_q = N`.
- At cycle T+3: `out_stg2_idx_q = N`. The case uses `mul0` (= value from cycle T+1's mul0_q = cycle T's operands). ✓

If I remove `mul` (the second register stage):
- At cycle T+1: `mul0_q = mul0_a * mul0_b`. `out_stg0_idx_q = N`.
- At cycle T+2: `out_stg1_idx_q = N`. The case uses `mul0_q` (= cycle T's operands). ✓

So the case should use `out_stg1_idx_q` (2 stages) instead of `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages). And `out_stg3_idx_q` (4 stages) for block_out writes needs to become `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages). But the block_out writes at `out_stg3` were 4 stages from input. If I make them 3 stages, they happen 1 cycle earlier, and the o_* registers (computed at the case stage, now 2 stages) need 1 more cycle to settle. So the block_out writes at 3 stages (from input) are 1 cycle after the case at 2 stages. That works!

Let me also adjust the valid_q pipeline. Currently `valid_q` starts from `out_stg3_valid_q` (4 stages) and delays 7 more stages (8-bit shift reg, output at [6]). Total 11 stages. If I remove 1 stage from the idx pipeline, `out_stg3` becomes 3 stages, and I need `out_stg3` to still be 4 stages from input for the valid_q timing to work. But if I reduce `out_stg3` to 3 stages, I need to add 1 more stage to valid_q to maintain 11 total. 

Actually wait - the valid_q[6] output timing is relative to when block_out entries are written. If block_out writes happen 1 cycle earlier (at 3 stages instead of 4), then the valid_q needs to be 1 cycle longer to maintain the same output timing. So I'd need valid_q to be 9 bits with output at [7]. Or I keep the same structure but start from a different point.

module jpeg_idct_transpose_ram
(
    input           clk0_i, input           rst0_i,
    input  [  4:0]  addr0_i, input  [ 31:0]  data0_i, input           wr0_i,
    input           clk1_i, input           rst1_i,
    input  [  4:0]  addr1_i, input  [ 31:0]  data1_i, input           wr1_i,
    output [ 31:0]  data0_o, output [ 31:0]  data1_o
);

reg [31:0] ram [31:0];
reg [31:0] ram_read0_q;
reg [31:0] ram_read1_q;

always @ (posedge clk0_i)
begin
    if (wr0_i)
        ram[addr0_i] <= data0_i;
    ram_read0_q <= ram[addr0_i];
end

always @ (posedge clk1_i)
begin
    if (wr1_i)
        ram[addr1_i] <= data1_i;
    ram_read1_q <= ram[addr1_i];
end

assign data0_o = ram_read0_q;
assign data1_o = ram_read1_q;

endmodule

    end
    3'd2: begin
        i0 <= block_in_0_1 - block_in_4_5;
    end
    3'd3: begin
        mul0_a <= block_in_0_1; mul0_b <= C7_16;
        mul1_a <= block_in_6_7; mul1_b <= C1_16;

        mul4_a <= i0;           mul4_b <= C4_16;
    end
    default: ;
    endcase
end

[ASSISTANT]
Now I have the full picture. Let me create design_v3 with the pipeline optimization. The key changes for both IDCT modules:
1. Remove the `mul` register stage (mul0..mul4)
2. Change case from `out_stg2_idx_q` to `out_stg1_idx_q` (using mul_q directly)
3. Change block_out writes from `out_stg3_idx_q` to `out_stg2_idx_q`
4. Change valid_q to start from `out_stg2_valid_q` and add 1 more stage to maintain timing
5. Remove `out_stg0` and `out_stg3` (no longer needed)

Wait, let me re-check the timing carefully. In the original:
- Case uses `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages from input) with `mul` (2 stages from mul_a/b, which is 1 stage from input = 3 stages from input)
- block_out writes use `out_stg3_idx_q` (4 stages from input), with o_* values computed at out_stg2 (3 stages)

In the new version:
- Case uses `out_stg1_idx_q` (2 stages from input) with `mul_q` (1 stage from mul_a/b, which is 1 stage from input = 2 stages from input) ✓
- block_out writes use `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages from input), with o_* values computed at out_stg1 (2 stages), 1 cycle before use ✓

Now for the valid_q: In the original, `out_stg3_valid_q` (4 stages) feeds into valid_q (7 more stages via shift register with output at [6]). Total 11 stages from input. The block_out writes happen at 4 stages (out_stg3). The valid output starts at 11 stages, which is 7 cycles after the block_out writes at idx=3. 

In the new version, block_out writes happen at 3 stages (out_stg2). So I need valid output at 10 stages (3+7). I need to shift from `out_stg2_valid_q` (3 stages) with 7 more stages. So `valid_q` is still 8 bits, but input is `out_stg2_valid_q` and output at [6] gives 3+7=10 stages. 

Wait, that's 10 stages, but the original was 11. Let me re-check the original: `out_stg3_valid_q` is 4 stages, `valid_q[6]` adds 6 more stages = 10 stages total? No: valid_q is 8 bits, the shift register shifts from [0] to [7], and output is at [6]. So `out_stg3_valid_q` → valid_q[0] (1c) → [1] (2c) → ... → [6] (7c). Total 4 + 7 = 11 stages. The first block_out write is at idx=3 (out_stg3, 4+3=7 cycles from block start), and valid output at 11 cycles from start of valid signal at idx=0 (cycle 4), so 4+7=11 from block start. ptr_q starts counting at 11 from block start, and block_out writes at 7,8,11 from block start (idx 3,6,7). The last write (idx=7, at 4+7=11) happens at the same time as ptr_q starts. ✓

In the new version: block_out writes at out_stg2 (3 stages), idx=3 at 3+3=6, idx=6 at 3+6=9, idx=7 at 3+7=10. I need valid_q output at the same time as the original (cycle 11 from block start). So `out_stg2_valid_q` (3 stages) + valid_q stages = 11 → need 8 stages of valid_q. That means valid_q is 9 bits with output at [7].

Hmm, that increases valid_q from 8 to 9 bits. So I save 5×32=160 FFs (removing mul) but add 1 FF to valid_q. Net saving per engine: 159 FFs. × 2 = 318 FFs. About 5% of area.

The pipeline optimization broke things. The issue is likely that the `valid_q` timing is wrong - block 0 passes (since it's the first block), but block 1 fails. This suggests the valid_q pipeline length doesn't match the original timing.

Let me reconsider. The issue might be that the valid_q delay is wrong. In the original:
- `out_stg3_valid_q` = 4 stages from input
- `valid_q[6]` = 4 + 7 = 11 stages from input (valid_q is 8 bits, output at [6], so 7 stages of delay from input to [6])

Wait: `valid_q <= {valid_q[6:0], out_stg3_valid_q}`. So valid_q[0] = out_stg3_valid_q (1 stage from out_stg3), valid_q[1] = valid_q[0] (2 stages), ..., valid_q[6] = valid_q[5] (7 stages from out_stg3). Total: 4 + 7 = 11 stages.

In my v3:
- `out_stg2_valid_q` = 3 stages from input
- `valid_q[7]` = 3 + 8 = 11 stages from input (valid_q is 9 bits, output at [7], so 8 stages)

That should be 11 stages, matching. But maybe the issue is that the block_out writes happen at different times relative to the valid output. Let me think more carefully...

In the original, block_out writes at `out_stg3_idx_q` (4 stages). The first write is at idx=3, so cycle 4+3=7 from block start. The valid output starts at 11 from block start. So the block_out data is available 4 cycles before streaming starts. The ptr_q counts from 0 when valid starts.

In v3, block_out writes at `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages). First write at idx=3, so cycle 3+3=6 from block start. Valid output at 11 from block start. So 5 cycles between write and stream. But the block_out[7] write at idx=6 happens at cycle 3+6=9 in v3 (was 4+6=10 in original). And block_out writes at idx=7 at cycle 3+7=10 (was 4+7=11 in original). The streaming starts at 11. So all writes complete before streaming starts. That should be fine.

The issue might be more subtle. Let me check: the o_* registers are computed at `out_stg1_idx_q` (2 stages in v3, was 3 stages at `out_stg2`). The block_out writes use o_* values at `out_stg2_idx_q` (3 stages in v3). So o_* is computed 1 cycle before use. That's the same as original (computed at 3 stages, used at 4 stages). ✓

Wait, but there's a cross-row dependency issue. The o_* values from one idx are used by a later idx. For example, o_t0 computed at idx=2 is used at idx=5. In the original, idx=2 at out_stg2 (3+2=5 cycles from block start), o_t0 is available at cycle 6. idx=5 at out_stg2 (3+5=8 cycles), uses o_t0. The gap is 3 cycles. In v3, idx=2 at out_stg1 (2+2=4 cycles), o_t0 available at cycle 5. idx=5 at out_stg1 (2+5=7 cycles), uses o_t0. Gap is 3 cycles. Same gap, should work. ✓

Hmm, let me check the mul pipeline more carefully. The issue might be that `mul_a/mul_b` are set at the same posedge as `inport_idx_i` changes, and `mul_q` captures the product one cycle later. But in the original, `mul_a/mul_b` are registers set based on `inport_idx_i` - they're set at the posedge AFTER `inport_idx_i` changes (since the case statement reads `inport_idx_i` combinationally and assigns to registered `mul_a/b`). So:

- Cycle T: inport_idx_i = N (available before posedge)
- Posedge T→T+1: mul_a/b updated based on N; out_stg0_idx = N
- Cycle T+1: mul_a/b = N's operands; out_stg0_idx = N
- Posedge T+1→T+2: mul_q = mul_a * mul_b; out_stg1_idx = N
- Cycle T+2: mul_q = N's product; out_stg1_idx = N

In v3, the case uses `out_stg1_idx_q` at cycle T+2, which equals N, and `mul_q` at cycle T+2 which is N's product. ✓

In original, the case uses `out_stg2_idx_q` at cycle T+3, which equals N, and `mul` at cycle T+3 which is N's product (mul = mul_q delayed by 1 = T+2's value = N's product). ✓

So the alignment should be correct. Let me check if the issue is with the `valid_q` and `ptr_q` timing. Maybe the ptr_q starts counting at the wrong time.

Actually, I think the problem might be that the `valid_q[7]` is 11 stages from input, but the original `valid_q[6]` was also 11 stages. However, the original had `out_stg3` (4 stages) → valid_q[0] (5) → ... → valid_q[6] (11). In v3, `out_stg2` (3 stages) → valid_q[0] (4) → ... → valid_q[7] (11). The timing should be the same.

But wait - there's a subtlety. The `valid_q` shift register advances every clock regardless of valid. In the original, the first valid input to valid_q is at `out_stg3` = 4 stages after the first input beat (idx=0). In v3, it's at `out_stg2` = 3 stages after. So the valid pulse enters valid_q 1 cycle earlier in v3. Then it takes 8 stages to reach [7] (was 7 stages to reach [6]). So the output valid is at 3+8=11 stages vs 4+7=11 stages. Same. ✓

But the ptr_q behavior might differ. In the original, ptr_q starts counting when `outport_valid_o` is high. In v3, same. The timing of when outport_valid_o first goes high should be the same (11 stages from first input beat of a block).

Hmm, but the issue is that the gap between blocks might matter. The x-pass pipeline has different latency in v3 (1 fewer stage). The x-pass output goes to the transpose, and the transpose has its own FSM. The x-pass output valid timing relative to the input changes.

In the original x-pass, `outport_valid_o = valid_q[6]` which is 11 stages from input. In v3, `outport_valid_o = valid_q[7]` which is also 11 stages. But wait - let me check the x-pass `ptr_q`. In the original, `ptr_q` starts at 0 and increments when `outport_valid_o` is high. `outport_data_o = block_out[ptr_q[2:0]]` and `outport_idx_o = ptr_q`. The first output is ptr_q=0, then 1, 2, ... 7, 0, 8, 9, ... 63. Actually, ptr_q is 6 bits and counts 0..63, wrapping. So the output idx goes 0,1,2,...,63.

This should be the same in v3. Let me check if maybe the block_out values are being overwritten before they're read. In the original, the x-pass streams 8 values per row (ptr_q 0..7 for row 0, 8..15 for row 1, etc.). But block_out only has 8 entries. So block_out[0..7] are written at idx=3/6/7, and then read out via ptr_q[2:0] as ptr goes 0..7, 0..7, ... 

Oh! The block_out is only 8 entries but outputs 64 values (ptr_q 0..63, using ptr_q[2:0] to index). This means the same 8 entries are read 8 times! That means the block_out holds the 8 values for one row, and they're streamed out, then the next row's 8 values are written and streamed, etc.

This changes everything. The timing between block_out writes and valid_q output is critical. Let me trace through:

For row 0 (input beats 0-7):
- Block start at cycle 0. Input idx 0 at cycle 0, ..., idx 7 at cycle 7.
- Original: out_stg3_idx = 0 at cycle 4, idx=3 at cycle 7 (writes block_out[0,3,4,7_tmp]), idx=6 at cycle 10 (writes block_out[7]), idx=7 at cycle 11 (writes block_out[1,2,5,6]).
- valid_q[6] goes high at cycle 11 (4+7=11 from idx=0's valid at cycle 0). ptr_q=0.
- Cycles 11-18: ptr_q goes 0..7, streaming block_out[0..7]. 

In v3:
- out_stg2_idx = 0 at cycle 3, idx=3 at cycle 6 (writes), idx=6 at cycle 9 (writes block_out[7]), idx=7 at cycle 10 (writes block_out[1,2,5,6]).
- valid_q[7] goes high at cycle 11 (3+8=11). ptr_q=0.
- Cycles 11-18: streaming. ✓

So the timing should be the same. The writes complete by cycle 10, streaming starts at cycle 11. ✓

But wait, what about the second row? Row 1 starts at cycle 8 (input beats 8-15, idx 0-7). In the original, the valid_q for row 1's output should start at cycle 8+11=19. In v3, also 8+11=19. ✓

Hmm, then why is it failing? Let me check if the `valid_q` initialization is correct. In the original, `valid_q` is 8 bits. When `img_start_i` pulses, valid_q is cleared. After that, the valid signal from `out_stg3_valid_q` starts shifting in. In v3, `valid_q` is 9 bits, cleared on `img_start_i`. The valid from `out_stg2_valid_q` starts shifting in 1 cycle earlier.

But the valid pulse from `out_stg2_valid_q` for the first input beat (idx=0, cycle 0) arrives at valid_q[0] at cycle 4 (3+1). In the original, `out_stg3_valid_q` for idx=0 arrives at valid_q[0] at cycle 5 (4+1). Then in v3, it reaches [7] at cycle 4+7=11. In original, reaches [6] at cycle 5+6=11. Same!

I think the issue might be with the `img_start_i` behavior or the gap between blocks. Let me check: when `img_start_i` pulses, both `valid_q` and `ptr_q` are reset. The `out_stg*` registers are only reset by `rst`, not `img_start_i`. So after `img_start_i`, the pipeline still has old data, but valid_q is cleared so no spurious outputs.

Actually, I wonder if the issue is that the valid_q in v3 has 1 extra bit, but the data pipeline (block_out writes) is 1 stage shorter. The gap between the last block_out write (cycle 10) and the start of streaming (cycle 11) is 1 cycle in v3, same as in original (write at 11, stream at 11). Wait, in the original, the last write (idx=7) is at cycle 11 and streaming also starts at cycle 11. That's the same cycle! So block_out[1,2,5,6] are written at the same posedge that ptr_q starts counting. The values are available because they're registered (the write happens at the posedge, and the read uses the registered value).

In v3, the last write (idx=7) is at cycle 10, and streaming starts at cycle 11. So there's a 1-cycle gap. The block_out values from cycle 10 are available at cycle 11. ✓

I'm confused about why it's failing. Let me check if maybe the issue is with the `o_*` register timing. The `o_s5` and `o_s6` are computed at idx=7 (default case) via `(o_t6_5 * 181) / 256`. In the original, this is at out_stg2 (cycle 3+7=10), and used at out_stg3 (cycle 4+7=11) for block_out writes. In v3, this is at out_stg1 (cycle 2+7=9), and used at out_stg2 (cycle 3+7=10) for block_out writes. So o_s5/o_s6 are available 1 cycle before use. ✓

Hmm, let me try a different approach. Maybe the issue is that the `block_out_tmp` write at idx=6 and the `block_out[7]` write from `block_out_tmp` at idx=6 are happening in the wrong order. In the original:
- idx=3 (cycle 7): block_out_tmp = (o_t0 - o_t7) >>> 11
- idx=6 (cycle 10): block_out[7] = block_out_tmp (from cycle 7)

In v3:
- idx=3 (cycle 6): block_out_tmp = (o_t0 - o_t7) >>> 11

Both have 3-cycle gap between tmp write and [7] write. ✓

Wait, I just realized the issue might be with the cross-row data dependency in the o_* registers. Let me look at the case more carefully:

At idx=2: `o_t0 <= o_t0 + o_t3` and `o_t3 <= o_t0 - o_t3`. These use o_t0 and o_t3 from previous idx writes. o_t3 was written at idx=0 (2 cycles earlier in original, same in v3). o_t0 was written at idx=1 (1 cycle earlier in original, 1 cycle earlier in v3). The key question: does the data from idx N reach the o_* register before idx N+2 reads it?

In the original:
- idx=0 at cycle 3: o_t3 = mul0+mul1 (available at cycle 4)
- idx=2 at cycle 5: reads o_t3 (from cycle 4) and o_t0 (from idx=1 at cycle 4, available at cycle 5)

Wait, idx=2 at cycle 5 reads o_t0 which was written at idx=1 (cycle 4), so it's available at cycle 5 (the write happened at the posedge from cycle 4 to 5). ✓

In v3:
- idx=0 at cycle 2: o_t3 written, available at cycle 3
- idx=2 at cycle 4: reads o_t3 (from cycle 3) and o_t0 (from idx=1 at cycle 3, available at cycle 4) ✓

OK the timing is the same. Let me just check if maybe I have a bug in the valid_q or ptr_q. Let me look at a simpler issue: maybe the y-pass valid_q needs to be 8 bits not 9, because the y-pass has different latency requirements.

Actually wait - for the y-pass, the input comes from the transpose, not directly from the input. The transpose output valid goes high for 64 consecutive cycles. The y-pass needs to output 64 values starting at a specific time relative to the transpose output. The y-pass `valid_q` timing needs to match.

In the original y-pass:
- `out_stg3_valid_q` (4 stages from transpose output) → `valid_q[6]` (7 more stages) = 11 stages from transpose output

In v3 y-pass:
- `out_stg2_valid_q` (3 stages) → `valid_q[7]` (8 more stages) = 11 stages from transpose output

Same total. But the y-pass `block_out` writes happen at `out_stg2` (3 stages) instead of `out_stg3` (4 stages). The streaming starts at 11 stages. In the original, the last write (idx=7) at cycle 4+7=11 and streaming at 11. In v3, last write at cycle 3+7=10 and streaming at 11. 

The y-pass outputs are checked against expected values at `outport_idx_o`. The y-pass `ptr_q` starts at 0 when `valid_q[7]` goes high and counts up. The expected idx is `{k[2:0], k[5:3]}` where k is the output count. My y-pass outputs `outport_idx_o = {ptr_q[2:0], ptr_q[5:3]}`. And `outport_data_o = block_out[ptr_q[2:0]]`.

Hmm, but the y-pass block_out only has 8 entries. For the y-pass, the output is 64 values. Each block_out entry is output 8 times (as ptr_q[2:0] repeats 0..7 for each group of 8). This means the y-pass writes 8 values per "row" and streams them 8 times. The y-pass needs to write new block_out values before the next group of 8 is streamed.

In the original y-pass, the block_out writes happen at `out_stg3` (4 stages from transpose input). The streaming starts at 11 stages. The gap is 11 - (4+7) = 0 for the last write (idx=7 at cycle 11). For the next group (row 1), the writes start at cycle 8+4=12 and the streaming for the previous group ends at cycle 11+8=19 (wait, that doesn't work).

Actually, I think the issue is more fundamental. Let me re-examine the y-pass output timing. The y-pass receives 64 beats from the transpose (64 consecutive valid cycles). It processes them through the pipeline and outputs 64 values. The block_out is written for each "column" (8 beats of input), and streamed 8 times.

The critical timing: the block_out for the next column must be written before the previous column's values are done streaming. 

In the original: column 0's block_out writes at out_stg3 (4 stages from transpose input beat 0). The streaming starts at valid_q[6] = 11 stages from beat 0. Column 0 streams for 8 cycles (11 to 18). Column 1's writes start at beat 8 + 4 = 12. Column 1 streams from beat 8 + 11 = 19 to 26. So column 0 streams 11-18, column 1 writes 12-19, column 1 streams 19-26. The write of column 1's block_out[7] at idx=6 is at beat 8+4+6=18, just before column 0 finishes streaming at 18. Wait, that's the same cycle!

Actually I think the key is that the block_out values for column 0 are all written before streaming starts, and column 1's values are written during column 0's streaming. Since block_out is only 8 entries, the writes for column 1 must not overwrite column 0's entries before they're done streaming.

Let me trace more carefully. The ptr_q goes 0..7 for column 0, reading block_out[0..7]. Column 0's streaming is cycles 11-18 (ptr_q = 0..7). Column 1's writes happen at out_stg3 = beat 8 + 4. So:
- Beat 8+3=11: write block_out[0,3,4,tmp] (column 1, idx=3)
- Beat 8+6=14: write block_out[7] (column 1, idx=6)
- Beat 8+7=15: write block_out[1,2,5,6] (column 1, idx=7)

Column 0 streaming: ptr_q=0 reads block_out[0] at cycle 11, ..., ptr_q=7 reads block_out[7] at cycle 18.
Column 1 writes: block_out[0,3,4] at cycle 11, block_out[7] at cycle 14, block_out[1,2,5,6] at cycle 15.

So block_out[0] is overwritten at cycle 11, but ptr_q reads block_out[0] at cycle 11! Since both happen at the same posedge, the read gets the OLD value (registered output) and the write updates for next cycle. ✓ But block_out[3,4] are also overwritten at cycle 11, and ptr_q reads block_out[3] at cycle 14 and block_out[4] at cycle 15. At cycle 14, block_out[3] has the NEW value from column 1 (written at cycle 11). But ptr_q=3 at cycle 14 should read column 0's block_out[3]! 

This means the original design has a race condition? No, because the block_out values are written with `out_stg3_valid_q` which might not be valid for all cycles. Let me re-check: `out_stg3_valid_q` is the delayed valid signal. For the transpose, the valid is high for 64 consecutive beats. So `out_stg3_valid_q` is high for 64 consecutive beats starting 4 cycles after the transpose starts. So the block_out writes happen for every idx cycle during those 64 beats.

This means the block_out IS overwritten while being read! The design must rely on the fact that the block_out values are read in order and the new values are written at the right time.

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating this. The original design works, and my v3 should have the same relative timing (just shifted by 1 cycle earlier for the writes). The issue might be something else entirely.

Good, design_v2 is confirmed working at 8.98M. The critical path is still the 32:1 RAM read mux (1400 ps from rd_addr_q Q).

Let me try a different approach to reduce the critical path: **add a pipeline register in the transpose RAM read path**. The idea is to pre-register the read address one cycle before it's needed. The transpose FSM has a SETUP state where it sets `rd_addr_q = 8`. If I can start the read one cycle earlier and register the output, I break the critical path.

Actually, let me think about this differently. The RAM read path is: `rd_addr_q` (register Q, 1400 ps through 32:1 mux) → `ram_read1_q` (register D). The 1400 ps is the mux. Let me try coding the RAM as a two-stage mux. I'll split the 32 entries into two groups of 16 (by addr[4]), mux each group (16:1), then select between them with addr[4] (2:1), with a pipeline register between the stages.
