[ASSISTANT]
design_v4 is identical to design_v2 (I just reorganized the code). Same cost. Now let me try a fundamentally different approach to break the critical path: **add a pipeline register to the read address in the transpose**. The idea is to compute the read address one cycle earlier and register it, so the RAM read mux starts from a pre-registered address. But the issue is that `rd_addr_q` is already registered. The mux delay comes AFTER the register.

The real fix: **register the RAM read data** with an extra pipeline stage. This means the read takes 2 cycles instead of 1. The transpose FSM currently has a SETUP state where `rd_addr_q = 8` is set, then ACTIVE where reads start. If I change to:
- SETUP: set `rd_addr_q = 8`, start first read
- ACTIVE: `ram_read1_q` is available (1 cycle after address)

Currently the read is: at cycle T, `rd_addr_q` has the address, and at the posedge T→T+1, `ram_read1_q` captures `ram[{block_rd_q, rd_addr_q}]`. The output is available at cycle T+1.

If I add a pipeline register: at cycle T, `rd_addr_q` → mux → `ram_intermediate_q` (registered). At cycle T+1, `ram_intermediate_q` → `ram_read1_q` (registered). This splits the 32:1 mux across 2 clock cycles. But `ram_intermediate_q` would need to be the mux output, which is the same as what feeds `ram_read1_q` now. Adding another register stage after the mux just adds latency without breaking the critical path.

No, the issue is: the critical path is `rd_addr_q` Q → 32:1 mux → `ram_read1_q` D. The mux is between two registers. To break it, I need to put a register IN the mux. That requires restructuring the mux.

What if I use a 2-level mux? First level: 4 groups of 8 entries (4:1 mux per group of 8, using addr[4:3]). Second level: 4:1 mux across the 4 groups (using addr[2:0]). If I register the first level output, the critical path becomes: `rd_addr_q` → 8:1 mux → register → 4:1 mux → `ram_read1_q`. Each mux is 8:1 (~350 ps) or 4:1 (~200 ps), much less than 1400 ps.

But this requires coding the mux explicitly with a pipeline register. Let me try:
  -> edit_file(filename='design_v4.v', new_string="module jpeg_idct_transpose_ram\n(\n    input           clk0_i, input           rst0_i,\n    input  [  4:0]  addr0_i, input  [ 31:0]  data0_i, input           wr0_i,\n    input           clk1_i, input           rst1_i,\n    input  [  4:0]  addr1_i, input  [ 31:0]  data1_i, input           wr1_i,\n    output [ 31:0]  data0_o, output [ 31:0]  data1_o\n);\n\nreg [31:0] ram [31:0];\n\n// Write port (port 0) - [ASSISTANT]
Good, design_v4 is back to 8.98M. Now let me try to reduce area. The `img_end_i` input is unused. Let me also try to reduce the `valid_q` shift register width. The x-pass `valid_q` is 8 bits with output at [6]. Let me check if I can use fewer bits. The valid_q provides a delay of 7 stages from `out_stg3_valid_q`. The output starts 7 cycles after `out_stg3_valid_q` first goes high. This is needed because block_out writes at out_stg3 idx=3,6,7 (cycles 7,10,11 from block start), and the streaming starts at cycle 11 (valid_q[6] = out_stg3 at idx=0 delayed by 7 = 4+7=11). 

The last block_out write (idx=7) is at cycle 4+7=11, same as when streaming starts. So the valid_q delay is exactly right. I can't reduce it.

Let me try to merge the `i0` register computation. The `i0` is computed at idx=0 (sum) and idx=2 (difference), then used at idx=1 and idx=5 (via mul4). It's needed because `i0` at idx=0 uses `block_in_0_1 + block_in_4_5` which are the EVEN coefficients from beat 0, and at idx=1 `mul4_a = i0` multiplies it by C4_16. But at idx=0, the inputs `block_in_0_1` and `block_in_4_5` are from the even set (c0, c4). At idx=2, they're the same even set. So `i0` alternates between sum and difference of the even coefficients. This is fundamental to the algorithm.

Let me try to reduce the multiplier width. The constants C1-C7 are 12-14 bits (799 to 4017). The input data in the x-pass is 16-bit signed (sign-extended to 32 bits). The product `block_in * C` would be at most 16+14 = 30 bits. But the spec says all arithmetic is 32-bit. The multiplication `mul0_a * mul0_b` is 32×32 → 32 bits (lower 32 bits, wrapping). If I narrow the operands, the product might differ. So I can't change the multiplier width.

Let me try a different approach: **reduce the number of transpose RAMs from 4 to 2** by using wider RAMs. Instead of 4 RAMs of 32×32, use 2 RAMs of 32×64. Each read gives 64 bits (2 values). But the y-pass needs 4 values per beat (data0-3), so I'd need to read both RAMs to get 4 values. The mux per bit would still be 32:1 but for 64-bit words. This doesn't help with mux depth.

Let me try yet another approach: **can I make the transpose read combinational (asynchronous) instead of registered?** If the RAM read is combinational, the path becomes: `rd_addr_q` → mux → y-pass input. The delay might change because the path endpoint changes.

Actually, if the read is combinational, the output goes directly to the y-pass's registered input. The critical path would be `rd_addr_q` → 32:1 mux → y-pass `block_in` register. This is still the same mux delay, but the endpoint is different. Let me try:

Let me check if there's actually overlap. The x-pass for block N+1 starts while the y-pass for block N is still running. The transpose double-buffers so both can run simultaneously. If I share the engine, I'd need to serialize x and y, which would require more buffering and change the throughput.

Let me try a simpler optimization: **reduce the transpose to single-buffered**. The testbench has 2+ idle cycles between blocks. If the y-pass can complete a block's output before the next block's input overwrites the buffer, single buffering works. But the y-pass outputs 64 values, and the next block's input starts ~2-4 cycles after the previous block's last input. The y-pass pipeline latency is ~11 cycles. Let me try a different approach: **change the transpose RAM read from a registered read to a write-first read-during-write** behavior. Actually, the read port never writes (wr1_i = 0), so the read is just a registered read. 

Let me try to restructure the transpose to **eliminate the FSM and use a simpler counter-based approach**. The FSM has IDLE → SETUP → ACTIVE. The SETUP state adds 1 cycle of latency. If I can eliminate SETUP and start reading directly, I might reduce the critical path... but that won't change the mux delay.

Let me try a completely different strategy: **reduce the data width of the RAM**. The transpose stores 32-bit values, but after `>>> 11` (x-pass), the result has at most 32-11 = 21 significant bits + sign = 22 bits. If I truncate to 22 bits, I save 10 bits × 32 × 4 = 1280 FFs. But the spec says all arithmetic is 32-bit, and the y-pass expects 32-bit inputs. Truncation would change the results.

Actually, let me re-read the spec: "ALL arithmetic is evaluated in 32-bit signed context with wrap-around; outputs of each pass are arithmetic-shifted right (11 then 15)." The x-pass output is `>>> 11`, and the y-pass input is 32-bit. The y-pass then does `>>> 15`. The intermediate values are 32-bit. I can't truncate.

Let me try to **merge the transpose FSM logic to reduce area**. The FSM uses 2-bit state_q, 6-bit rd_idx_q, 4-bit rd_addr_q, 2-bit block_ready_q, 1-bit block_wr_q, 1-bit block_rd_q. That's 16 FFs. Not much to save there.

Let me try another approach: **reduce the IDCT engine area by sharing some registers**. The x-pass and y-pass each have 13 o_* registers (o_s5, o_s6, o_s7, o_t0-t7, o_t6_5, o_t5_6 = 13 × 32 = 416 FFs) plus 10 mul_a/b registers (320 FFs), 5 mul_q (160 FFs), 5 mul (160 FFs), 8 block_out (256 FFs), 1 block_out_tmp (32 FFs), valid_q (8 FFs), ptr_q (6 FFs), 4 idx pipeline stages (8 FFs), i0 (32 FFs). Total per engine: ~1398 FFs. × 2 = 2796 FFs. Plus 4 RAMs (4096 FFs) + transpose logic (~20 FFs) = ~6912 FFs total.

The RAMs are 4096/6912 = 59% of FFs. The IDCT engines are 2796/6912 = 40%.

Can I reduce the RAM from 4096 to 2048 FFs by using single buffering? I showed that doesn't work due to overlap. But what if I add a small FIFO to decouple the timing? That would add FFs, not reduce them.

Let me try a radical approach: **eliminate the transpose entirely by feeding the x-pass output directly to the y-pass with a different addressing scheme**. The x-pass produces 8 values per row (8 rows = 64 values). The y-pass needs 8 values per column (8 columns = 64 values). The transpose corner-turn can be eliminated if I restructure the y-pass to process row-by-row instead of column-by-column. But the 2D IDCT requires processing columns after rows, and the column processing needs all 8 row results for each column.

Actually, the fundamental issue is that the 2D IDCT requires a matrix transpose between the row and column passes. This requires storing the entire 8×8 block.

Let me try yet another approach: **use the y-pass `block_out` registers as the transpose buffer**. The y-pass has `block_out[0:7]` (256 FFs) that holds 8 values. If I restructure so the y-pass reads directly from a 64-entry buffer instead of the 4-RAM transpose, I might reduce the total buffer size.

Wait, the y-pass `block_out` is only 8 entries and it's reused for each column. It's not a 64-entry buffer. The transpose IS the 64-entry (×2 for double buffering) buffer.

Let me try to think about this problem from the cost perspective. The cost is 4264 × 2105 = 8.98M. The original baseline was 4265 × 2509 = 10.7M. My improvement (the y-pass idx fix) reduced delay from 2509 to 2105 (16% reduction). 

Can I further reduce the delay? The 2105 is the RAM read mux. The only way to reduce it is to reduce the mux depth (fewer entries) or add pipeline stages (which changes timing).

Let me try the pipeline approach again, but this time properly compensating for the extra latency. I'll add a pipeline register in the RAM read and adjust the transpose FSM to start reading 1 cycle earlier.

The transpose FSM: IDLE → SETUP → ACTIVE. In SETUP, `rd_addr_q = 8` is set. In ACTIVE, reads start with `rd_idx_q = 0`. The read result is available 1 cycle later (synchronous read). So the first data is available at ACTIVE cycle 1 (rd_idx_q = 1).

If I add a pipeline register, the read takes 2 cycles. So I need to start reading during SETUP. In SETUP, set `rd_addr_q = 8` and start the read. The first data is available at ACTIVE cycle 0 (rd_idx_q = 0). This would align with the original timing (data available when outport_valid goes high in ACTIVE).

Let me try: I'll add the pipeline register inside the RAM, and modify the FSM to set `rd_addr_q = 8` during IDLE→SETUP transition (so the first read starts during SETUP). But the address needs to be available during SETUP for the first read to complete by ACTIVE cycle 0. Let me restructure:

Actually, the current FSM already sets `rd_addr_q = 8` during SETUP. The read at SETUP uses this address, and the result is available at ACTIVE cycle 0. But the `outport_valid_o` is only high during ACTIVE. So the data from the SETUP read is available at the start of ACTIVE.

With the pipeline register, the read during SETUP gives data at ACTIVE cycle 1 (2 cycles after SETUP). So I'd need to start the read during IDLE→SETUP transition. That means setting `rd_addr_q = 8` during IDLE, not SETUP.

This is getting complex. Let me try a simpler approach: just make the read address available 1 cycle earlier by precomputing it. I'll register `rd_addr_q` one cycle ahead:

[ASSISTANT]
Good, back to 8.960M. Now let me try to merge the `block_out` output mux and the transpose to see if I can save the `block_out` register array in the x-pass. 

Actually, let me try to reduce the x-pass `block_out` by using the `valid_q` to generate the output index directly. The x-pass streams `block_out[ptr_q[2:0]]` and `outport_idx_o = ptr_q`. The block_out is 8 entries of 32 bits = 256 FFs. If I could eliminate this by having the transpose capture the values directly...

The x-pass output goes to the transpose's `inport_data_i`. The transpose writes it to RAM at `inport_idx_i[5:4]` bank, position `inport_idx_i[3:0]`. The x-pass `outport_idx_o = ptr_q` which goes 0..63. So `inport_idx_i[5:4]` = ptr_q[5:4] = upper 2 bits of the stream position. `inport_idx_i[3:0]` = ptr_q[3:0] = lower 4 bits.

So the x-pass block_out values are stored in the transpose RAM. The block_out register array is just a temporary buffer between the IDCT computation and the transpose write. Can I write directly to the transpose RAM instead of block_out?

The issue is that the IDCT produces 4 values at specific idx cycles (3, 6, 7), but the transpose expects 1 value per cycle in order 0..63. The block_out holds the 8 values and streams them out in order. Without block_out, I'd need to produce the values in order 0..7, which requires a different computation schedule.

This is too complex. Let me try a simpler optimization: **reduce the number of constant multipliers**. The constants C1-C7 are used as mul_b operands. In the x-pass, mul0_b takes C2, C1, C7, C7, C6 across different idx cycles. mul1_b takes C6, C7, C1, C1, C2. These are constant values, so the multiplier is effectively a constant multiply. Yosys might optimize constant multipliers to shifts+adds, which could be smaller than a general 32×32 multiplier.

But wait, the mul_b values are stored in registers (mul0_b, mul1_b, etc.), so Yosys sees them as variable multipliers. If I make the constants part of the case statement directly (not through registers), Yosys might optimize better. But the spec says "5 multipliers over an 8-beat schedule", so the design uses 5 time-shared multipliers.

Actually, the multiplier inputs go through registers (mul_a, mul_b → mul_q). The mul_b values are constants loaded into registers. Yosys sees `mul0_a * mul0_b` where both are registered, so it can't optimize the constant. If I restructured to compute `block_in * C1` directly (constant in the multiply), Yosys could use a smaller multiplier. But that would change the pipeline structure.

Let me try a different approach: **make the mul_b constants part of the case statement** by computing the products directly in the case. Instead of:
```
mul0_a <= block_in_2_3; mul0_b <= C2_16;
...
mul0_q <= mul0_a * mul0_b;
```

Do:
```
mul0_q <= block_in_2_3 * C2_16;
```

This makes the constant visible to Yosys for optimization. But the mul0_q register would be loaded from different constant multiplies depending on the case. Let me try this:

## Summary

### What Worked Best
The best result (cost 8.96M, down from baseline 10.7M) came from **two key changes** in `design_v5.v`:
1. **Replaced the y-pass `ptr_conv` lookup table function** with a direct bit-swap `{ptr_q[2:0], ptr_q[5:3]}`. This eliminated a 64-entry case-based function that generated significant combinational logic, reducing both area and delay (2509→2105 ps).
2. **Removed unused `img_end_i` and `INPUT_WIDTH` parameter** from submodule ports, yielding a small area reduction.

### What Had the Most Impact
The `ptr_conv` → bit-swap change alone reduced cost by ~16% (10.7M → 8.98M), as it removed a large mux/LUT structure that was either on or near the critical path.

### What Didn't Work / Caused Regressions
- **Split RAM into two 16-entry banks**: Made delay *worse* (2105→2246 ps). Yosys's native 32:1 mux optimization was better than an explicit 16:1 + 2:1 split.
- **Removed `ram_read0_q` (unused write-port read register)**: Worsened delay significantly (2105→2457 ps) — the second read port helped Yosys share mux decode logic.
- **Removed `mul` pipeline stage** (merging mul_q→mul): Broke correctness — the 4-stage idx pipeline was needed to align with the 2-stage mul pipeline.
- **Pipelined 2-stage RAM read**: Broke correctness (extra cycle of read latency misaligned transpose→y-pass timing).
- **Combinational (async) RAM read**: Broke correctness — y-pass expects registered data.
- **Flatten attribute `(* flatten *)`**: Made delay worse (2105→2509 ps) — hierarchical optimization was better.
- **Address bit remapping** (moving ±8 bit to MSB): Made delay worse (2105→2420 ps).
- **Inlined constant multiplies** (removing mul_a/mul_b register stage): Broke correctness — the mul_a/mul_b registers were needed to hold operands across the schedule, and removing them changed the pipeline alignment.

### Lessons Learned
1. **The transpose RAM 32:1 read mux is a hard structural bottleneck** (~1400 ps) that cannot be reduced without changing the fundamental buffer architecture or adding pipeline stages that break the carefully tuned timing.
2. **Yosys's mux optimization is surprisingly good** — manually restructuring muxes (split-bank, bit-remapping) consistently made things worse.
3. **The design has very tight pipeline timing** — every register stage serves a specific alignment purpose. Removing or merging stages almost always breaks correctness.
4. **The best ROI came from eliminating unnecessary combinational logic** (the ptr_conv LUT) rather than trying to restructure the datapath.
5. **`target_delay` had no effect** on this design since the critical path was structurally fixed by the RAM mux.
